My dad was always reading the newspaper. The TV was always tuned to the news. It was just a sense like that's the most important thing. Whatever's happening in the world is the most important thing that you ought to be paying attention to out there.  I don't know, it was a great upbringing with great parents and a good neighborhood and good friends. You know, I felt both a sense of belonging and a sense of being an outsider kind of at the same time. And I had this name that nobody could pronounce. And we're in this gray neighborhood. People did all kinds of different things. Our neighbor to one side laid carpet. A family across from us were retired public school teachers. The lady on the corner was a nurse. And I'd see her walk down to the night shift at the hospital a couple blocks away every day.  and I felt like the texture of that neighborhood, like really belonged to me. But at the same time, like I also felt and knew that I was like, I was different. Like this was before it was like trendy to drink espresso, for example. They just couldn't quite innovate and keep up and the company collapsed. That was a catastrophic thing for the city. The city lost a big chunk of its population and never really recovered it.  And when I was a kid, I didn't really know any different. I didn't realize it was unusual that factories that hadn't been operating for decades, in some cases, that looked like they'd been hit in a war. And the message that I got, the kind of lesson that I was given was, I don't know if anybody said this to me in a sentence, but what the world kind of said to me was, if you want to be something, you want to be someone, you have to get out.
